## Page 1


On the Symmetries of Anisotropic Spin Interaction Models
Arist Zhenyuan Yang1, ∗
1Department of Physics and HK Institute of Quantum Science & Technology,
The University of Hong Kong, Pokfulam Road, Hong Kong, China
(Dated: May 15, 2026)
We show that anisotropic spin interactions do not merely break spin-space group (SSG) sym-
metries, but instead twist them through cohomology invariants, yielding symmetry classes beyond
subgroups of O(3) × Isom(R3). This requires redefining the spin-only group S0 in terms of proper
spin rotations. Based on this unitary S0, we formulate a twisted SSG (tSSG) theory that captures
the complete set of spin-space symmetries. We then study a spin-1 model with tSSG symmetry using
linear flavor wave theory and find topological quadrupolar excitations defined on a spin Brillouin
Klein-bottle rather than the conventional torus. Specifically, the bosonic BdG Hamiltonian satisfies
a glide reflection sewing relation, the ribbon spectrum exhibits M¨obius boundary states.
These
topological excitations are classified by Z2, enforced by the nonorientability of the Klein-bottle.
Introduction.—
Spin-space groups (SSGs) provide a
unifying symmetry framework for magnetic materials,
enabling systematic descriptions of magnetic topological
electronic states [1–4], topological magnon bands [5–13],
and the altermagnetism [14–21]. These systems are gov-
erned by isotropic Heisenberg exchange in which spin
and spatial operations combine in a particularly trans-
parent manner: each symmetry operation is realized as
a global spin rotation in O(3) accompanied by a lat-
tice isometry, and the resulting SSGs form subgroups of
O(3) × Isom(R3) [22–27].
In realistic magnets,
anisotropic spin interactions
generated by spin-orbit coupling are ubiquitous and
often essential [28–33].
Canonical examples include
Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) exchange [34, 35], compass-
type bond interactions [36–39], and single-ion anisotropy
originating from the crystal field [40–42]. These terms
are commonly assumed to reduce or preserve the sym-
metry of isotropic magnets [43].
However, we propose
the model [Eq. (1)] to show that anisotropy instead
changes the type of symmetry realized: exact symme-
tries of anisotropic models involve spatially varying spin
rotations, so that the full symmetry group cannot em-
bed into O(3) × Isom(R3). We further find that these
spin symmetries acquire a precise group-theoretic mean-
ing within group extension theory only after removing
effective time-reversal from the spin-only group S0.
Motivated by these findings, we formulate a theory
of twisted spin-space groups (tSSGs) that completes the
symmetry description of spin systems via group exten-
sion theory. The key step is to redefine S0 as the group
of on-site proper spin rotations that are exact symme-
tries of the system. With this definition, we show that
any spin-space operation g admits a natural decompo-
sition into (i) a lattice operation lg (unitary or antiuni-
tary), (ii) a global normalizer spin operation Rg ∈O(3)
that implements the global conjugation action on S0,
and (iii) a site-dependent centralizer sector Zg(⃗r) that
commutes with S0 at every site.
If Zg(⃗r) ≡1, then
g ∈O(3) × Isom(R3), and such operations necessarily
form a subgroup of O(3) × Isom(R3). In contrast, when
there exists an element g with nontrivial centralizer sec-
tor Zg(⃗r), the group structure, as will shown below, is
modified by a two cocycle [ω2] ∈H2
ϕ(GL, Z(S0)). Here
GL denotes the set of lattice operations {lg, ∀g} and
ϕ : GL →Aut(Z(S0)) is the action induced by conju-
gation on the center of S0 via the normalizer sector Rg.
Remarkably, tSSGs give rise to a novel class of topo-
logical magnetic excitations on nonorientable manifolds.
We study a two-dimensional anisotropic spin-1 model
[Eq. (13)] on the wallpaper group pm using linear flavor
wave theory.
The Hamiltonian contains uniform XYZ
anisotropy and DM interactions on x bonds, uniform off-
diagonal symmetric anisotropy and DM interactions on
y bonds, and single-ion anisotropy D(Sz
i )2. For large D,
the {Qxz, Qyz} sector is defined not on the conventional
Brillouin torus but on a Brillouin Klein-bottle [44–46].
Specifically, in this sector, the bosonic BdG Hamiltonian
satisfies the glide reflection sewing relation
UHBdG(kx, ky)U −1 = HBdG(−kx, ky + π).
Moreover, the two edge modes obey a nonlocal twist
ωL(ky) = ωR(ky + π), sharply distinguishing them from
conventional topological magnetic excitations.
We also present a general scheme for models realizing
tSSGs based on the cocycle free construction of group
extensions, and provide a complete classification of spin
point groups with S0 valued in the 11 chiral point groups,
summarized in the Supplementary Material (SM).
Taken together, these results open a route to unconven-
tional magnetism on nonorientable spin Brillouin mani-
folds and, more generally, spin Brillouin platycosms.

We first analyze the unitary symmetries of the model.
Since the space group (SG) p4mm is symmorphic, it ad-
mits the decomposition p4mm ≡C4v ⋉(Tx × Ty), where
C4v is generated by {C4, Mx}.
The Wyckoff position
1b = (1/2, 1/2) is fixed by C4v, and Tx and Ty denote

With this notation, the Hamiltonian is invariant under
five global unitary operations: spin rotations SO(2)z =
{Rz(θ)}, two effective translations (Rx(π) || Tx,y), and
two effective mirror reflections (Rx(π) || Mx,y). These
symmetries are all contained in collinear SSGs.
An exotic unitary symmetry arises when the lattice ro-
tation C4 is spin-decorated to become a symmetry of the
Hamiltonian. The resulting effective rotation necessarily
exchanges the XY interaction on x-bonds with the DM

We now demonstrate the nontrivial nature of

in comparison with conventional spin point groups

The site-dependent spin operations Zg(⃗r) in eC4 and eT
modify the cohomology invariants and generate symme-
try groups absent in the conventional formulation. This
mechanism originates from the nontrivial conjugation of
SO(2)z by Rx(π), which necessitates decomposing the
full symmetry group with respect to the unitary sub-
group SO(2)z, rather than the spin-only group O(2)z. A
decomposition based on O(2)z instead obscures this con-
jugation structure and the associated cohomology invari-
ants. This choice is further enforced by the fact that the
conventional antiunitary operation T ′ ∈O(2)z is broken
and replaced by the site-dependent symmetry eT .
Basic Setup.—
Below,
we redefine the spin-only
group and formulate a basic conceptual framework for
symmetries in spin systems.
Detailed constructions of
tSSGs are presented in the following section.
A spin-only group is defined as a subgroup of SO(3)
and is denoted by S0. Representative examples of S0 in-
clude the 11 chiral point groups (cPGs) consisting solely
of proper rotations: 6 abelian groups C1, C2, C3, C4, C6,
and D2, and 5 nonabelian groups D3, D4, D6, T , and O.
Continuous groups such as SO(2)z, SO(3) also fall into
this category. In the following, we denote by Z(S0) the
center of S0, and by NS(S0) and ZS(S0) its normalizer
and centralizer in a group S ⊂O(3), respectively.
For a given spin Hamiltonian, the spin-only group S0
is defined as the set of all on-site global proper rotations
that leave the Hamiltonian invariant. By definition, S0 is
a normal subgroup of the full symmetry group G. Then
G admits a coset decomposition,
G = S0 ⊔u1S0 ⊔u2S0 ⊔· · · ⊔T1S0 ⊔T2S0 ⊔· · · ,
(5)
where u1, u2, . . . are unitary elements and T1, T2, . . . are
antiunitary elements.
These cosets themselves form a
group isomorphic to a magnetic space group (MSG) GL,
equivalently G/S0 ∼= GL. Conversely, the complete class
G of symmetry groups for spin systems with a given S0
is naturally defined as
G = {G | G/S0 ∼= MSG} .
(6)
The set G splits into two classes. One class consists of
groups that can be embedded as subgroups of the direct
product O(3) × Isom(R3); the other does not admit such
an embedding. All elements of G are referred to as tSSGs,
including cases with nontrivial cohomology twists such
as G3 defined in Eq. (3). Groups belonging to the first
class are, by convention, also referred to as SSGs. Two
tSSGs G, G′ ∈G are defined to be physically equivalent
if there exists a spin transformation s ∈O(3) such that
G = s G′ s−1. Equivalently, the two groups are related by
a global change of spin coordinates.
However, the definition in Eq. (6) is overly general and
may include groups G that are not physically meaning-
ful for spin systems. For example, when S0 = SO(2)z,
the nontrivial conjugation action on S0 is not unique to
the spin flipping Rx(π), but can also be realized by other
unitary matrices, such as U = diag(iσx, 1) ∈SU(3). Al-
lowing such conjugations would lead to an SU(3) theory,
which lies outside the scope of spin physics. We there-
fore impose the following restriction: the conjugation ac-
tions of any operations in G ∈G on S0 must be realized
by elements of O(3). Under this condition, G defines a
complete class of tSSGs relevant to spin systems. In the
following, G refers exclusively to this restricted class.
Twisted Spin-Space Groups.—
We now construct
tSSGs. The construction is abstract, and the representa-
tive example introduced above will be used to clarify the
key ideas when appropriate. In what follows, G denotes
a tSSG with spin-only group S0, satisfying G/S0 ∼= GL,
where GL is a MSG.
We show that the structure underlying the operation
eC4 = (ZC4Rx(π) || C4) is generic to all tSSGs.
Ow-
ing to the global nature of S0, each element g ∈G ad-
mits a systematic decomposition into three sectors. Let
sg(⃗ri) ∈O(3) denote the spin operation implementing
the conjugation action of g on S0 at site ⃗ri. Since el-
ements of S0 act globally, the conjugation action of g
preserves S0 at all sites, implying
sg(⃗ri) S0 sg(⃗ri)−1 = sg(⃗rj) S0 sg(⃗rj)−1 = S0.
(7)
Thus, sg(⃗ri) and sg(⃗rj) normalize S0, and their differ-
ence sg(⃗ri)sg(⃗rj)−1 lies in the centralizer of S0. Fixing
a reference site ⃗r0, each element g ∈G can therefore be
written as
g =

We now present a universal construction of models re-
alizing tSSGs. This mechanism is rigorously supported
by the cocycle free construction of group extensions [50].
In general, tSSGs with any given S0 can arise from the
coexistence of on-site single-ion anisotropy Hamiltoni-
ans Hsite, with spin-only group S0, and bond spin in-
teractions Hbond, whose spin-only group is Z(S0), see
Fig. 2. In this setting, the group homomorphism ˆR is
fixed by the symmetries of Hsite, while Hbond admits site-
dependent sector valued in ZSO(3)(S0) ⊆ZSO(3)
 Z(S0)

.
FIG. 2. The Hamiltonian (12) realizing tSSGs with arbitrary
S0. The upper layer represents the spin-exchange Hamilto-
nian Hbond, while the lower layer represents the on-site single-
ion anisotropy Hamiltonian Hsite.
A Hamiltonian with such tSSG symmetry therefore takes
the generic form
H = λ1Hsite + λ2Hbond,
λ1, λ2 ∈R.
(12)
In the limit λ2 →0, the model reduces to one whose
symmetries form an SSG with spin-only group S0 and no
centralizer sector. In contrast, for λ1 →0, it reduces to
a model whose symmetries form a tSSG with spin-only
group Z(S0), with the centralizer sector taking values in
ZSO(3)(S0). Rigorous proofs are provided in the SM.
Topological quadrupolar bands on spin Brillouin Klein-
bottle.—
As an application of the tSSG theory, we show
that spin models protected by tSSGs can yield quadrupo-
lar excitations on a spin Brillouin zone whose fundamen-
tal domain is topologically a Klein-bottle, arising from
the presence of a free action, namely a glide reflection
in the spin Brillouin zone [44]. This Klein-bottle topol-
ogy reshapes the topological classification of quadrupolar
excitation bands [51, 52]: all such bands are equivalent
to bands on the Klein-bottle. We refer to these excita-
tions as topological quadrupolars on spin Brillouin Klein-
bottle. These magnetic excitations are classified by Z2,
since nonorientability of Klein-bottle identifies 1 = −1.
Consider the following anisotropic spin-1 Hamiltonian
with S0 = C2 on the lattice ⃗r = (rx, ry) ∈Z × Z with
wallpaper group pm:

which counts the number of roots of q(z; ky) inside the
unit disk.
This Z2 value is the slice topological in-
variant that stabilizes edge localization: any symmetry-
preserving perturbation that leaves the glide sewing in-
tact and keeps q nonzero on the unit circle cannot change
ν(ky) and hence cannot remove the corresponding bound-
ary mode.
For instance, set λ = 1 and γ = 0.9, the
condition |γ sin ky| < |λ| holds for every ky, so all slices
are topological and the M¨obius pair spans the full spin
Brillouin zone rather than a fragmentary edge arc.
Discussion and Outlook.—
Since DM vectors act as a
gauge field for spin currents [53], the site-dependent cen-
tralizer sector Zg(⃗r) acquires a natural interpretation as
a spin gauge. In this view, tSSGs arise from gauging the
centralizer of S0. Such gauge-dressed symmetries, known
as projective crystalline symmetry [45, 54], can generate
topological phases on Brillouin platycosms [46]. Realiz-
ing such phases in solid state materials remains challeng-
ing. The topological quadrupolar excitations on the spin
Brillouin Klein-bottle studied here provide a first model
in magnetic systems.
This motivates the search, both
theoretically and experimentally, for topological mag-
netic excitations on general spin Brillouin platycosms.

S1. REFORMULATION OF CONVENTIONAL SSGS
Using the formulation developed in the main text, conventional SSGs are readily recovered. For classical
magnetic orders, the unitary spin-only group S0 falls into three cases: SO(3), SO(2)z, and the trivial group
C1, corresponding to nonmagnetic systems, collinear magnetic order, and coplanar or noncoplanar magnetic
order, respectively. The latter two are distinguished by the presence or absence of an effective time-reversal
symmetry. Nonmagnetic systems require (−E || T ) to be a symmetry, whereas collinear magnetic order
requires (−Rx(π) || T ) to be a symmetry.
The classification of SSGs then reduces to computing the group homomorphisms
ˆR : GL →NO(3)(S0)/S0,
(S1)
subject to the constraint that antiunitary elements are mapped to spin operations with determinant −1. The
different cases are discussed separately below.
1. Nonmagnetic systems.
When S0 = SO(3), the center is trivial, Z(SO(3)) = C1, and
NO(3)(SO(3))/SO(3) = Z−
2 = {E, −E}.
Accordingly, the group homomorphism ˆR admits two possibilities, depending on whether GL contains
antiunitary elements. If GL is a space group, ˆR is trivial. If GL is a magnetic space group, ˆR is uniquely
fixed by requiring its kernel to be the maximal unitary subgroup of GL. For a given ˆR, the corresponding
SSG G is given by
G = {(SO(3) ˆR(g) || g) | g ∈GL}.
In this case, the presence of the symmetry (−E || T ) implies G ∼= O(3) × SG.
2. Collinear magnetic order.
When S0 = SO(2)z, the normalizer quotient is
NO(3)(SO(2)z)/SO(2)z = Zx
2 × Z−
2 = {E, Rx(π), −E, −Rx(π)}.
(S2)
Accordingly, the group homomorphism ˆR decomposes as follows: unitary elements of GL are mapped to
{E, Rx(π)}, while antiunitary elements are mapped to {−E, −Rx(π)}.
For collinear magnetic order, the effective time-reversal symmetry is fixed as ˆR(T ) = −Rx(π). The
classification of ˆR therefore reduces to that of one-dimensional real representations of the maximal unitary
subgroup of GL, equivalently, of a crystallographic space group.
3. Coplanar magnetic order.
We assume coplanar spin moments lying in the x–y plane. In this case,
NO(3)(C1)/C1 = O(3).
(S3)
Coplanar systems admit the antiunitary symmetry (−Rz(π) || T ), which fixes ˆR(T ) = −Rz(π). Since the spin-
only group is trivial, this effective time-reversal operation commutes with all symmetry operations. This implies
that the image of ˆR restricted to the maximal unitary subgroup of GL lies in diag[g, det(g)] ⊂SO(3) with
g ∈O(2)z. Consequently, the classification reduces to enumerating the two-dimensional real representations
of the maximal unitary subgroup of GL.
4. Noncoplanar magnetic order.
For noncoplanar magnetic order, the spin-only group is trivial, S0 = E,
and no antiunitary symmetry is present. Consequently, GL is an ordinary space group. Since
NO(3)(C1)/C1 = O(3),
(S4)
the corresponding homomorphism ˆR is specified by a three-dimensional real representation of GL.
We conclude this section by addressing symmetry twists in conventional SSGs. Since neither SO(3) nor the
trivial group C1 has a nontrivial center, the associated second cohomology groups are trivial. Consequently,
twisted SSGs with nontrivial cohomology invariants can arise only in collinear magnetic systems, as illustrated
by the examples discussed in the main text.

S3. GROUP EXTENSIONS WITH ARBITRARY KERNELS AND AN ALTERNATIVE
CONSTRUCTION OF TWISTED SSGS
Consider the following group extension
1 →S0 →G →GL →1.
(S8)
Below, we present three complementary constructions for G: one for abelian kernels S0 and two for nonabelian
kernels S0. The latter two consist of a cocycle-dependent construction, which directly leads to the formulation
of twisted SSGs in the main text, and a cocycle-free construction, which proves that twisted SSGs with
nonabelian spin-only groups arise generically from the coexistence of on-site single-ion anisotropy and bond
spin-exchange interactions. For a standard introduction to group extension theory, see Serre’s book [? ].
We begin with the case in which the spin-only group S0 is abelian. A group homomorphism ˆR : GL →

The construction above is cocycle-dependent. We now present a complementary cocycle-free construction
of nonabelian group extensions. This approach is based on two group extensions with kernels S0 and Z(S0),
respectively. We keep G0 as defined above, and let F denote a group extension of GL by Z(S0). Consider
the diagonal subgroup ∆GL ⊂GL × GL, and define G0 ×GL F be the inverse image of ∆GL under the map
G0 × F →GL × GL. The group G0 ×GL F is called the product of G0 and F over GL. It fits into the exact
sequence
1 →S0 × Z(S0) →G0 ×GL F →GL →1.

FIG. S1. Decomposition of the total Hamiltonian into on-site and bond interaction terms.
Let ∆−= {(s, s−1) ∈S0 × Z(S0)}. Then ∆−is a normal subgroup, and (S0 × Z(S0))/∆−∼= S0. Define
G := (G0 ×GL F)/∆−. This yields an extension
1 →S0 →G →GL →1,
(S16)
which is isomorphic to the twisted SSG G constructed from the cocycle ω2 ∈H2
ϕ(GL, Z(S0)).
The cocycle-free construction has a direct physical application. It shows that twisted SSGs with nonabelian
S0 arise generically from the coexistence of on-site single-ion anisotropy with spin-only group S0 and bond
spin-exchange interactions with spin-only group Z(S0). In general, consider Hamiltonians of the form
H = Hsite + Hbond,
where the on-site term Hsite has spin-only group S0, while the bond term Hbond has spin-only group Z(S0),
as illustrated in Fig. S1. Let G0 and F denote the symmetry groups of Hsite and Hbond, respectively. In the
absence of additional constraints, the symmetry of the two seperated systems Hsite and Hbond are the direct
product G0 × F, and the spin-only group is S0 × Z(S0). In order to obtain the symmetry group G of the full
Hamiltonian H, two constraints must be imposed. First, the two subsystems must share the same lattice
operations. Second, their on-site symmetry operations must coincide. Accordingly, the direct product group
G0 × F must be restricted to the fiber product G0 ×GL F, and on-site operations (s, b) ∈S0 × Z(S0) are
required to be trivialized whenever s · b = 1. The two constraints uniquely fix the symmetry group G of the
full Hamiltonian H = Hsite + Hbond. In particular, G coincides precisely with the group extension defined in
Eq. (S16).

Theorem 1 (M¨obius sewing relation) The semi-infinite solutions derived above obey
µL(ky) = µR(ky + π),

right edge at momentum ky + π.
The theorem combined with the bulk glide sewing, this is the precise sense in which the {Qxz, Qyz} sector
ribbon spectrum is a Klein-bottle rather than a torus.

There is, however, an important distinction in the present bosonic model. When β ̸= 0, the full fixed-ky
slice is no longer strictly chiral because of the diagonal τz term in Eq. (S75). Therefore ν(ky) is most precisely
interpreted as the invariant of the auxiliary off-diagonal chiral block q. On the exactly solvable line, this
auxiliary invariant remains the correct existence invariant of the bosonic ribbon mode, because the β term
only shifts the edge energies to µL,R(ky) = D ± 4β cos ky without changing the root z∗(ky) or the decay
factor. In this sense, ν(ky) is the slice topological invariant that controls the nontrivial edge localization,
while the full glide-enforced bosonic statement is the Moebius sewing relation ωL(ky) = ωR(ky + π).
As an example, set λ = 1 and γ = 0.9, hence
|γ sin ky| ≤0.9 < 1 = |λ|
for all ky.
(S85)
Therefore every fixed-ky slice has ν(ky) = 1, and the exact edge branch exists throughout the entire spin
Brillouin zone. This is the analytic reason the ribbon spectrum realizes a complete Moebius pair rather than
a fragmentary edge arc.
S5. ENUMERATION OF SPIN-POINT GROUPS G SATISFYING G/S0 = PG × ZT
2
A. Algorithmic workflow.
The classification proceeds as follows:
1. Point group input. Construct the point group PG explicitly as a finite subgroup of O(3) from a small
generating set.
2. Choice of spin only group S0. Fix one of the 11 admissible chiral point groups,
S0 ∈{C1, C2, C3, C4, C6, D2, D3, D4, D6, T , O} ⊂SO(3).
(S86)
3. Normalizer in SO(3). Determine NSO(3)(S0) (see Table S1). For the discrete nonaxial cases S0 ∈
{D2, D3, D4, D6, T , O}, the normalizer is finite and constructed explicitly. For the axial cases S0 ∈
{C2, C3, C4, C6}, one has NSO(3)(S0) ∼= O(2), which is continuous but admits a finite representative set
sufficient for crystallographic enumeration. For the trivial case S0 = C1, NSO(3)(C1) = SO(3), and the
continuous target is handled via the list of crystallographic finite rotation groups.
4. Quotient target (see Table S1). Construct
Q0(S0) = NSO(3)(S0)/S0.
(S87)
For continuous normalizers, replace the quotient by a finite representative target capturing all crystallo-
graphically admissible images.
5. Enumeration of group homomorphisms. Enumerate all maps
ˆR : PG →Q0(S0)
(S88)
by brute force over generator images. Candidates are first filtered by order constraints, Order


|

Assignments producing inconsistent values are rejected.
6. Reduction to conjugation inequivalence. Quotient the raw set by spin transformations. For finite Q0,
ˆR ∼ˆR′ ⇐⇒∃q ∈Q0 :
ˆR′(lg) = q ˆR(lg) q−1
